- Five-Justice comparison, liability and remedy requirements: [Holder v. Hall — 2. A reasonable comparison must have legal and practical roots independent of a desired racial tally; 3. The accepted comparison does not replace the required proof of unequal opportunity; 4. The existing remand must provide a lawful remedy for the established violation](https://github.com/Alex-Oss222/scotus-career/blob/main/terms/OT1993/records/Holder_v_Hall_merits_1994-06-30.md#public-projection), 1994-06-30. Blackmun's Opinion of the Court, Part II, joined by Stone-Zsela, Stevens, Souter and Ginsburg; five Justices directly adopt the complete comparison rule and its application. O'Connor and Kennedy reject that comparison; Thomas and Scalia reject dilution coverage on a different, broader ground. Blackmun's Opinion of the Court, Part III, joined by Stone-Zsela, Stevens, Souter and Ginsburg; five Justices directly adopt the stated application and preservation of the existing proof requirements. There is no separate holding that the Gingles predicates alone establish liability or shift the ultimate statutory burden. Blackmun's Opinion of the Court, Part IV, joined by Stone-Zsela, Stevens, Souter and Ginsburg; the five-Justice judgment coalition adopts these remedial limits and the affirmance of the Eleventh Circuit's existing remedy remand.

**Present operation effective:** Holder v. Hall, 1994-06-30.

**Related current rules:** “Section 2 fragmentation, state districting choice and influence claims” supplies the related preconditions and the limits on maximization and proportionality.

**Holdings navigation:** [The county's governing-body size is within Section 2's coverage](https://github.com/Alex-Oss222/scotus-career/blob/main/state/HOLDINGS.md#the-countys-governing-body-size-is-within-section-2s-coverage); [A reasonable comparison must have legal and practical roots independent of a desired racial tally](https://github.com/Alex-Oss222/scotus-career/blob/main/state/HOLDINGS.md#a-reasonable-comparison-must-have-legal-and-practical-roots-independent-of-a-desired-racial-tally); [The accepted comparison does not replace the required proof of unequal opportunity](https://github.com/Alex-Oss222/scotus-career/blob/main/state/HOLDINGS.md#the-accepted-comparison-does-not-replace-the-required-proof-of-unequal-opportunity); [The existing remand must provide a lawful remedy for the established violation](https://github.com/Alex-Oss222/scotus-career/blob/main/state/HOLDINGS.md#the-existing-remand-must-provide-a-lawful-remedy-for-the-established-violation).

## Earlier current-term public law and published positions

Verbatim selections from workspace/neutral-projection.md. All selected decisions predate June 29, 1995. Same-day Rosenberger and Sweet Home are excluded. Chabad requires refresh after the expressly sequenced Pinette decision.

### Lebron v. National Railroad Passenger Corp., No. 93-1525. — 1995-02-21

Authority: [Record](../records/Lebron_v_National_Railroad_Passenger_Corp_merits_1995-02-21.md#public-projection); public substance is copied in the [generated chunk handoff](../render-inputs/OT_1994CHUNK2.md).

### The Court may consider this included legal argument despite its earlier disavowal

**Controlling proposition:** The Court elects to consider Amtrak's governmental-entity status despite Lebron's earlier express disavowal of that argument because it supplies a legal basis for the same preserved First Amendment claim, falls within the governmental-actor question presented, and can be resolved on the established institutional record without a new factual theory or additional findings. This bounded exercise of review creates no obligation to consider every abandoned argument and does not dispense with limits on distinct claims, questions, requested relief, factual stipulations, or missing material facts.

**Authority:** Justice Scalia's reachability portion, joined by Justices Stevens, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer. Seven Justices adopt the stated discretionary-review rationale; Chief Justice Stone-Zsela reaches the question but does not join this portion, and Justice O'Connor declines the argument as a prudential matter.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that the earlier litigation choice deserves consideration but does not require refusal of this legal argument. Yee separates a new supporting argument from a distinct claim and independently enforces the question presented. Lebron's injury, constitutional claim, rejected advertisement, and requested relief remain the same. The question before the Court expressly concerns governmental status despite the statutory disclaimer, and the established corporate structure permits its resolution without new factual findings. Bray withheld a separate statutory claim outside the granted dispute and lacking a necessary finding; those defects are absent here. Deliberate disavowal is more substantial than mere omission, but the Court exercises its authority to decide this included legal basis on the present record. The appellate reliance on Amtrak's private status makes that premise relevant without establishing a rule that any passing discussion preserves every argument. No actual adversarial prejudice or broader concession is found, and the Court imposes no burden on the respondent to prove prejudice.

**Precedent treatment:**

- Yee v. City of Escondido, 503 U.S. 519 (1992): applies its distinction between a supporting argument and a new claim, together with its independent question-presented limit; the present decision separately addresses deliberate disavowal in this setting.
- Bray v. Alexandria Women's Health Clinic, 506 U.S. 263 (1993): distinguishes the unadjudicated separate hindrance theory, outside the granted questions and lacking a necessary finding; its scope limits remain operative.
- Bankers Life & Casualty Co. v. Crenshaw, 486 U.S. 71 (1988): applies, through Yee, the allowance of supporting arguments for a preserved federal claim, without erasing this Court's discretion over deliberately excluded grounds.
- Kamen v. Kemper Financial Services, Inc., 500 U.S. 90 (1991): preserves the distinction between legal arguments and the claim to which they relate; no unrelated claim is introduced.
- United States v. Mezzanatto, decided January 18, 1995: distinguishes the actual, limited evidentiary agreement there from this decision whether to consider an included argument; its waiver rule does not make every litigation choice an irrevocable surrender of an entire constitutional claim.

### Special federal creation and permanent majority appointment make Amtrak governmental

**Controlling proposition:** When the Federal Government creates a corporation by special law to advance governmental objectives and permanently retains authority to appoint a majority of its governing directors, the corporation is governmental for First Amendment purposes notwithstanding a statutory declaration that it is not a governmental agency. Amtrak satisfies those combined conditions; ordinary operating discretion in its corporate officers does not require a separate external federal command for each challenged act.

**Authority:** Justice Scalia's constitutional-status portion, joined by Chief Justice Stone-Zsela and Justices Stevens, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer. Eight Justices directly support the same permanent-majority-appointment rule and its application; Justice O'Connor's conditional view of a question she declines to reach is not a join.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that Congress cannot settle constitutional responsibility simply by choosing a corporate label. Amtrak was specially created to pursue national transportation objectives, and the statutory arrangement permanently retains governmental authority over selection of its governing majority. The Secretary's position, the five other direct Presidential selections, and the preferred shareholder's governmental selections must not be mistaken for nine direct Presidential appointments. The different routes nevertheless establish the required enduring majority authority; the corporate president's selection within the institution does not defeat it. Congress's nonagency classification keeps its statutory operation, but cannot place an institution created and governed in this manner beyond the Constitution. San Francisco Arts & Athletics distinguishes a federally chartered and assisted private corporation; charter, subsidy, or regulation alone is not the ground here. Nor does delegation of everyday decisions to corporate officers make this institution private. An external federal order to reject Lebron's work is therefore unnecessary. The Court decides governmental status, leaving the advertising policy's constitutionality and the suitable remedy for later adjudication.

**Precedent treatment:**

- San Francisco Arts & Athletics, Inc. v. United States Olympic Committee, 483 U.S. 522 (1987): distinguishes federal charter and assistance to a private institution from special creation coupled with permanent governmental majority-appointment authority.
- Edmonson v. Leesville Concrete Co., 500 U.S. 614 (1991), and Georgia v. McCollum, 505 U.S. 42 (1992): preserve attention to actual governmental legal machinery in their jury-selection settings; neither makes billboard advertising an exclusively governmental function or supplies a general rule for all publicly benefited corporations.
- Jackson v. Metropolitan Edison Co., 419 U.S. 345 (1974), and Blum v. Yaretsky, 457 U.S. 991 (1982): distinguish attribution of a private entity's particular act from identifying an institution that is itself governmental under the combined rule.
- Hess v. Port Authority Trans-Hudson Corp., decided November 14, 1994: keeps the distinct inquiry into an entity's state sovereign immunity separate; absence of immunity does not imply constitutional privacy.
- American National Red Cross v. S. G., 505 U.S. 247 (1992): preserves its express charter-jurisdiction rule, which supplies no constitutional actor classification.

**Limits and questions not reached:** Federal charter, subsidy, regulation, governmental property, public benefit, a single appointment, temporary investment, or merely the current ownership of shares is not independently sufficient under this rule. Permanent authority to appoint the governing majority, governmental objectives, and special governmental creation must be present together. The Court decides no universal agency status, sovereign immunity, Treasury obligation, federal tort-liability rule, or charter jurisdiction. The 1994 recodification from former 45 U.S.C. §541 to 49 U.S.C. §24301(a) does not itself adjudicate constitutional status. The statutory board includes the Secretary of Transportation; Amtrak's president; three Presidentially appointed, Senate-confirmed directors representing the statutory labor, state-executive, and business categories; two Presidential selections from commuter-authority nominees under the statute; and two preferred-shareholder selections. The governmental preferred shareholder supplies those last two selections on this record. Neither the entire board's consideration of this particular refusal nor a new direct federal order to reject it is found.

### Governmental status requires a remand, not an immediate display order

**Controlling proposition:** Reversal of the Second Circuit's governmental-action ground restores the First Amendment claim for adjudication with Amtrak treated as governmental, but does not establish that this rejection was unconstitutional or that Lebron is entitled to mandatory display. The lower courts must address the unreviewed speech and forum questions, lawful relief, and the related disposition of the contract claim and TDI counterclaim under their own governing rules; the district display injunction is not automatically reinstated.

**Authority:** Justice Scalia's remand portion, joined by Chief Justice Stone-Zsela and Justices Stevens, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer. Eight Justices adopt the same threshold reversal and limited remand.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that governmental responsibility identifies the institution's constitutional duties without deciding whether the challenged restriction violates them. The Court of Appeals stopped at attribution and did not adjudicate the First Amendment merits. The district court's contrary decision and mandatory-display order therefore cannot be restored merely by correcting that threshold. The character of the advertising forum, the grounds and operation of the speech restriction, and the conditions for equitable relief remain separate questions. Young preserves the distinction between the existence of substantial federal jurisdiction and the legal defeat of a federal claim; restoration of the claim accordingly requires reconsideration of associated state-law dispositions without prescribing their answers. Tome illustrates only the limited principle that correcting one ground does not automatically establish ultimate relief; its hearsay and criminal-error rules do not govern this civil remand. No damages amount, contract entitlement, forum classification, or validity of a substitute advertising policy is determined here.

**Precedent treatment:**

- Young v. Northern Illinois Conference of United Methodist Church, decided January 17, 1995: applies the distinction between statutory jurisdiction and legal defeat on the federal merits without extending its ministerial-selection defense.
- Tome v. United States, decided January 10, 1995: uses only its separation of threshold legal error from automatic ultimate relief by analogy; no evidentiary or criminal harmless-error standard is transferred.

**Remedial limits:** The Court awards no display, damages, final contractual relief, or adjudication of TDI's requested declaration permitting termination. The Second Circuit's dismissal directions must be reconsidered consistently with the restored federal claim and ordinary law governing the separate state-law issues. The lower courts may not treat this actor-status ruling as a merits judgment approving the advertisement or invalidating every political-advertising policy.

Yee and Bray preserve the distinction between a legal supporting argument, a separate claim, and the actual question presented; the Court exercises bounded discretion over this deliberately disavowed argument. San Francisco Arts & Athletics, Jackson, and Blum retain their private-actor scope. Edmonson and McCollum concern governmental jury machinery. Hess addresses immunity and Red Cross charter jurisdiction, neither of which settles constitutional actor status. Young and Tome support only the stated separation of thresholds, merits, and relief. No earlier holding is overruled.

Effective February 21, 1995, special federal creation for governmental objectives combined with permanent federal authority to appoint a governing majority establishes First Amendment governmental status despite a statutory nonagency label. That rule governs Amtrak on this record without requiring an external governmental command for the particular refusal. The decision also permits consideration of this included legal argument despite its earlier disavowal on the stated claim, question, and record conditions, without a universal rule requiring review of abandoned arguments. The advertising merits and final remedies remain unresolved.

### McIntyre v. Ohio Elections Commission, No. 93-986 (current petitioner: executor of Margaret McIntyre's estate). — 1995-04-19

Authority: [Record](../records/McIntyre_v_Ohio_Elections_Commission_merits_1995-04-19.md#public-projection).

#### Holdings

### Compelled identification on these political leaflets requires exacting scrutiny

**Holding and operative rule:** A content-defined requirement that an individual identify herself on ordinary political issue leaflets burdens core political expression, including the choice to speak anonymously. The State must satisfy exacting scrutiny by showing narrow tailoring to an overriding state interest; viewpoint neutrality or the election setting alone does not lower that standard.

**Authority:** Justice Stevens's opinion of the Court, joined in full by Chief Justice Stone-Zsela and Justices O'Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer: seven direct rationale votes. Justice Thomas concurs only in the judgment on his distinct anonymous-political-pamphlet ground; Justice Scalia dissents.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that the author's choice to omit her identity is protected as part of political expression. Talley protects anonymous handbills and recognizes the place of anonymous publication in public discussion. Ohio's requirement selects publications by their electoral subject and conditions their circulation on identification. It is therefore a content-based burden on the individual's communication, not simply a neutral rule for administering a ballot. Burson preserves demanding scrutiny for campaign-specific restrictions even though its different polling-place judgment had no shared validity rationale. Ohio invokes the modest size of the identification obligation and its election-related purpose. Neither answers whether the State may impose the burden on this ordinary issue leaflet. The Court applies exacting scrutiny requiring narrow tailoring to an overriding interest. It does not decide that anonymity is always benign or require proof that this speaker faced retaliation. Candidate finance reports, organizational attribution, coordinated spending, and fraudulent impersonation present distinct questions left outside this holding.

**Precedent treatment:**

- Talley v. California, 362 U.S. 60: applies protection of anonymous handbills to this individual political leaflet; the Court does not establish immunity for fraudulent communications.
- Burson v. Freeman, May 26, 1992: applies the independently majority-supported scrutiny distinction; its fractured polling-place validity judgment does not settle this leaflet requirement.
- Anderson v. Celebrezze, 460 U.S. 780: distinguishes the election-administration setting; it does not reduce scrutiny for every election-related speech restriction.

### The asserted voter-information interest does not justify this identification demand

**Holding and operative rule:** A general interest in helping voters evaluate a source does not justify requiring this ordinary individual to place her name and residence or business address on her independently produced, truthful political leaflet. The Court leaves candidate finance, campaign committees, organizational spending, and other materially distinct disclosure settings for separate analysis.

**Authority:** Justice Stevens's opinion of the Court, joined in full by Chief Justice Stone-Zsela and Justices O'Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer: seven direct rationale votes. Justice Thomas concurs only in the judgment on his distinct anonymous-political-pamphlet ground; Justice Scalia dissents.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that voters' interest in source information does not justify this direct condition on an ordinary citizen's political pamphlet. Talley protects the decision to publish without personal attribution; an author need not establish that identity has no possible informational value. Ohio relies on Buckley's campaign-finance disclosure and Bellotti's discussion of identifying corporate advertising. Those sources concern different mechanisms and interests. A report identifying financial participation in a campaign is not the same requirement as putting a private individual's name and address on every issue leaflet she distributes. Corporate spending likewise supplies no automatic rule for this record, which establishes neither a campaign organization nor coordinated financing. The Court does not hold that source information is useless or that all disclosure is forbidden. It holds that the general informational rationale is inadequate for the burden imposed here. Ohio's committee alternatives and personal-correspondence exception do not transform Margaret McIntyre's independently produced leaflets into those different statutory categories or justify this sanction.

**Precedent treatment:**

- Talley v. California, 362 U.S. 60: applies protection for the ordinary author's choice of anonymous publication.
- Buckley v. Valeo, 424 U.S. 1: distinguishes campaign-finance reporting and its specific interests; those disclosure holdings remain undisturbed.
- First National Bank of Boston v. Bellotti, 435 U.S. 765: distinguishes corporate-advertising source information; its observation does not resolve this private individual's leaflet.

### Fraud prevention does not justify this application to truthful independent advocacy

**Holding and operative rule:** Although preventing electoral fraud is a weighty interest, Ohio may not impose this categorical identification requirement on truthful, independently produced individual issue leaflets without the necessary narrow fit to that interest. The enforcement order and $100 fine must be set aside; properly tailored fraud, impersonation, and materially different disclosure rules remain available.

**Authority:** Justice Stevens's opinion of the Court, joined in full by Chief Justice Stone-Zsela and Justices O'Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer: seven direct rationale votes. Justice Thomas concurs only in the judgment on his distinct anonymous-political-pamphlet ground; Justice Scalia dissents.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that Ohio's fraud-prevention interest does not supply the missing fit. Talley protects anonymous expression without preventing laws directed at false or fraudulent communications. Ohio's identification requirement reaches ordinary truthful advocacy regardless of any finding of deception, false attribution, or corrupt financing. The record contains no adjudicated fraud in McIntyre's leaflet. The State argues that identification helps investigators find wrongdoers and deters unlawful claims before they affect an election. That practical benefit does not explain why every individual issue pamphlet must carry a name and address when existing laws address fraud and false attribution more directly. The personal-correspondence exception and permission to exempt impractical small items do not cure this application to printed leaflets; no implementing exemption rule resolves the fine presented. The Court neither assumes that anonymity guarantees honest speech nor invalidates all disclosure provisions. It sets aside this sanction, preserves properly tailored enforcement against actual abuse, and leaves organizational and financial-reporting requirements outside the judgment.

**Precedent treatment:**

- Talley v. California, 362 U.S. 60: applies anonymous-publication protection while distinguishing measures directed at fraud.
- Buckley v. Valeo, 424 U.S. 1: leaves candidate-finance disclosure and corruption-related interests intact rather than treating them as dispositive of this ordinary leaflet.
- First National Bank of Boston v. Bellotti, 435 U.S. 765: leaves materially different corporate source-disclosure questions outside the individual sanction decided here.

#### Precedent Treatment

Talley's anonymous-handbill protection applies to this individual's political issue leaflet. Burson's majority-supported scrutiny distinction remains separate from its fractured validity judgment concerning polling-place environs. Anderson's election-administration framework does not decide this direct speech burden. Buckley's campaign-finance disclosure holdings and Bellotti's corporate-advertising discussion concern materially different interests and mechanisms and remain undisturbed. No precedent is overruled.

#### Law After Decision

Effective April 19, 1995, the State may not enforce this name-and-residence-or-business-address requirement against the truthful, independently produced individual political leaflets at issue. Seven Justices directly adopt exacting scrutiny and the inadequate information and fraud-prevention justifications; Thomas supplies a separate eighth judgment vote. No Marks synthesis is needed. The decision leaves candidate and committee disclosures, coordinated expenditures, large financial contributions, organizational spending, fraudulent impersonation, and appropriately tailored fraud regulation outside the adjudicated application. It does not create an automatic personal-correspondence or small-item exemption, decide an unproduced exemption rule, or adjudicate subsection (B).

### Adarand Constructors, Inc. v. Peña, No. 93-1841. - 1995-06-12

Authority: [Record](../records/Adarand_Constructors_Inc_v_Pena_merits_1995-06-12.md#public-projection)

#### Holdings

### Equal competitive opportunity and prospective standing

**Holding and operative rule:** A bidder able and ready to compete suffers a personal injury when a governmental racial preference denies equal competitive opportunity; it need not prove that it would have received the contract. Prospective relief also requires a real, sufficiently imminent renewed exposure to that preference, which Adarand's repeated bidding and continuing opportunity to encounter the challenged incentive establish here.

**Authority:** Threshold portion of Justice Stevens's opinion of the Court, joined by Chief Justice Stone-Zsela and Justices Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer; five Justices adopt the proposition. Justices O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy and Thomas independently agree with this threshold conclusion in their dissenting positions.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that unequal opportunity to compete is an injury even when the award remains uncertain. Northeastern Florida identifies the governmental barrier, rather than proof of the award that would follow its removal, as the relevant competitive injury. Adarand submitted the low bid, but the prime contractor selected a certified disadvantaged business in response to the financial incentive. The contractor could decline that incentive; its optional character does not remove its effect on competitive opportunity. Lujan requires the connection to be concrete and the requested relief appropriate to a continuing or threatened injury. Adarand's recurring bids and the likelihood of encountering the challenged clause again satisfy that prospective requirement on this record. The Court does not infer a guaranteed future award, an entitlement to damages, or a waiver of sovereign immunity. Nor does accepting this competitive injury establish that the challenged preference is unconstitutional; justification remains a separate question.

**Precedent treatment:**

- Northeastern Florida Chapter, AGC v. Jacksonville (June 14, 1993): applies its equal-opportunity injury rule; no guaranteed contract award is required.
- Lujan v. Defenders of Wildlife (June 12, 1992): retains concrete injury, causal connection and stage-appropriate proof; prospective relief requires sufficiently imminent renewed exposure.

### The inherited federal remedial standard sustains this challenge

**Holding and operative rule:** Under Metro Broadcasting, a benign federal racial classification supported by Congress must serve an important governmental objective and be substantially related to that objective; Fullilove retains its supported judgment recognizing Congress's remedial authority at the scope actually sustained there. Applying that meaningful review of purpose, fit and burdens, the Court affirms the judgment rejecting this preserved challenge to the subcontractor compensation arrangement, without deciding every certification route or conferring a general exemption on measures called benign.

**Authority:** Merits portion of Justice Stevens's opinion of the Court, joined by Chief Justice Stone-Zsela and Justices Souter, Ginsburg and Breyer; five Justices adopt this rule and bounded application, not merely the judgment.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that supported efforts to overcome exclusion differ constitutionally from measures maintaining racial subordination. Metro Broadcasting supplies the inherited federal standard, and Fullilove recognizes Congress's distinct authority and competence to identify and remedy barriers to economic participation. Those decisions do not authorize preferences on goodwill alone. The lower court examined the statutory evidence, delegation, eligibility and flexibility, rather than simply accepting a remedial label. The incentive remains optional, qualifying firms outside the presumptive groups may obtain certification, and the challenged presumptions are subject to safeguards and rebuttal. Those features support the judgment within the challenge presented. The Court does not treat the lower court's description of concessions as an unconditional waiver: the actual appellate statement accepted evidence only for discussion and continued to contest tailoring, and the merits brief contests the delegation characterization. Nor does the Court find that Gonzales used a particular certification route. Croson's treatment of state and local classifications does not require abandonment of the governing federal precedents here.

**Precedent treatment:**

- Metro Broadcasting, Inc. v. FCC, 497 U.S. 547 (1990): retains the important-objective/substantial-relationship standard for benign federal classifications at its actual scope.
- Fullilove v. Klutznick, 448 U.S. 448 (1980): retains its supported remedial judgment and congressional-competence recognition; no single universal rationale is attributed to the fractured decision.
- City of Richmond v. J. A. Croson Co., 488 U.S. 469 (1989): distinguished as state/local remedial classification law; its standard is not displaced or extended to erase Metro here.
- Wygant v. Jackson Board of Education, 476 U.S. 267 (1986): retains attention to evidence, remedial fit and burdens; remedial purpose alone supplies no exemption.

#### Precedent Treatment

Metro and Fullilove remain effective at the scope stated in Holdings; Croson and Wygant retain their separate settings and burdens. Shaw remains territorial-design law at its adopted scope; it establishes no universal contracting scrutiny tier. No precedent is overruled.

#### Law After Decision

The inherited federal remedial framework remains controlling; the preserved challenge is rejected. The decision does not decide every certification route, all future applications, sovereign immunity, damages, or any program-wide injunction. The compensation clause is an optional incentive, distinct from STURAA's expenditure command or a mandatory subcontracting duty. DBE subcontracts must total more than 10% of the original prime contract; compensation is 10% of approved final DBE subcontract amounts, capped at 1.5% of the original contract for one DBE and 2% for two or more. It compensates the stated assistance and documentation duties and requires certification evidence and a certified executed subcontract copy. Compensation is confined to DBE-performed work; adjustment may apply to non-DBE second-tier work, with full compensation permitted where only insignificant portions are subcontracted and the DBE performs the major portion. No finding is made that every duty was performed or every payment is constitutional.

SBA §8(a) presumes social disadvantage but requires individual proof of economic disadvantage. DOT state certification rebuttably presumes both, and third parties may challenge actual disadvantage. The §8(d) regulations remain in tension: 13 CFR §124.106(b) appears to require individual economic proof while 48 CFR §19.703(a)(2) appears to presume both. Nonminority applicants may establish disadvantage. Other governmental, county or city certification is accepted individually only on the contracting officer's finding of an acceptable and viable certification program. Mandatory §8(a) review, urged but nonmandatory DOT state review, and the absence of an identified separate §8(d) review provision remain distinct. Gonzales's actual route is unknown. The Court does not find irrebuttability, lack of actual disadvantage, or resolve the conflicting §8(d) rules.

### Missouri v. Jenkins, No. 93-1823 - 1995-06-12

Authority: [Record](../records/Missouri_v_Jenkins_merits_1995-06-12.md#public-projection)

#### Holdings

### Violation-linked salary support is permissible on the preserved findings

**Holding and operative rule:** A desegregation court may fund instructional and noninstructional salary support reasonably necessary to deliver educational measures repairing established segregation-caused injury, without treating low salaries as a separate constitutional wrong. These challenged orders are sustained on their independently sufficient intradistrict educational-repair and personnel-necessity findings; incidental voluntary attraction does not negate that ground, and the broader magnet foundation is not reopened.

**Authority:** Souter's opinion of the Court, joined by Stone-Zsela, Stevens, Ginsburg and Breyer; five Justices adopt this holding and affirm both reviewed salary judgments.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that these salary orders rest on a sufficient lawful educational-repair basis. Milliken II permits measures repairing educational effects of state-imposed segregation, while Swann makes equitable flexibility serve the established wrong. The preserved findings identify inferior education, reduced achievement and the staff necessary to implement remedial instruction and programs. Critical support functions include maintenance, warehouse, operating, mailroom and food-service work. The State's argument that each salary shortfall must itself be unconstitutional mistakes implementation costs for the original injury. Nor does voluntary attraction outside the district defeat an independently sufficient repair basis within it. Milliken I still bars a remedy for an unproved interdistrict violation; the Court neither authorizes external racial balancing nor reopens the magnet foundation without adequate notice and briefing. The November salary findings were not challenged as clearly erroneous; distinct December labor-market and support-staff challenges were reviewed under that standard. The Court preserves those distinctions, approves no automatic future increase and orders no general educational improvement guarantee.

**Precedent treatment:** Milliken v. Bradley (Milliken II), 433 U.S. 267, is applied to violation-linked educational repair and necessary implementation. Swann is applied to equitable means bounded by the adjudicated injury. Milliken I, 418 U.S. 717, retains the interdistrict-violation limit; it is not extended into a categorical support-staff exclusion.

### Partial release requires the complete Freeman showing

**Holding and operative rule:** Partial withdrawal may occur only after the defendant seeking release proves full and satisfactory compliance and practicable elimination of the relevant vestiges, whole-decree good faith shown by conduct, and the necessary causal and remedial connections; retained control necessary or practicable for compliance elsewhere and nonobstruction of remaining relief must be assessed. Missouri’s proof of installing programs does not make that complete showing, so continued funding and denial of partial release are affirmed; low scores alone establish neither continuing constitutional injury nor completed compliance, and national parity is not an indispensable release condition.

**Authority:** Souter's opinion of the Court, joined by Stone-Zsela, Stevens, Ginsburg and Breyer; five Justices adopt the complete existing Freeman rule and its present-record application.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that Missouri did not establish entitlement to the requested partial release. Dowell identifies the temporary purpose of desegregation relief, and the Court's Freeman decision permits withdrawal in stages only on its complete showing. Installing programs and spending funds do not alone prove practicable elimination of the effects those measures repair. Missouri's submission also did not establish its own whole-decree good faith, the remaining remedial connections or causal independence. Achievement evidence can illuminate those questions, but below-national scores are neither an automatic finding of constitutional injury nor a command to continue every beneficial program permanently. The contrary argument assumes that implementation itself ends the obligation; it omits the effects and connections Freeman requires. Conversely, the Court does not adopt maximum educational potential or satisfaction of every program goal as the constitutional standard. A later motion remains available on proper evidence. Existing findings receive clear-error review, and a finding on one issue cannot resolve unexamined causal or remedial relationships. No additional consensus, reviewer endorsement or changed-circumstances requirement is imposed.

**Precedent treatment:** Freeman v. Pitts, 503 U.S. 467, is applied at the completed Court decision’s operative scope: permission, release and causal burdens, whole-decree conduct, retained control and nonobstruction. Dowell is applied to temporary relief and dissolution. Rufo retains its consent-modification scope and supplies no changed-circumstances condition for fulfilled school obligations.

The State and district’s respective responsibilities must be assessed separately; one defendant’s conduct does not establish the other’s compliance. The defendant bears the release showing and the showing that imbalance for which it disclaims responsibility is not traceable to the violation. Genuinely independent demographic change imposes no perpetual balancing duty once the required work is completed. Racial imbalance alone proves neither liability nor completed remedy. Partial release may, rather than must, follow a satisfactory showing. The court must examine whether retaining control is necessary or practicable for compliance elsewhere, avoid obstructing remaining relief, and define released duties, remaining obligations and retained supervision. Existing factual findings receive clear-error review; findings on one issue do not decide unexamined connections. There is no all-components-at-once restriction or good-intentions substitute.

#### Precedent Treatment

The holdings apply Milliken II, Swann, Milliken I, Dowell and Freeman as stated. Rufo is kept within its distinct modification scope. No precedent is overruled. Ex parte Peterson and La Buy appear only in the concurrence and establish no new majority procedural holding here.

#### Law After Decision

Violation-linked educational repair includes supported instructional and critical support-staff implementation. The complete Freeman rule remains controlling without a national-score-parity requirement. The broad magnet foundation is reserved on this review. The judgment does not guarantee general improvement, perpetual funding or immediate release. Shaw’s racial-design-only districting holding neither removes the independently established segregation injury nor decides school-remedy scope.

### Hurley v. Irish-American Gay, Lesbian and Bisexual Group of Boston; No. 94-749 — 1995-06-19

Authority: [Record](../records/Hurley_v_Irish_American_Gay_Lesbian_and_Bisexual_Group_of_Boston_merits_1995-06-19.md#public-projection).

#### Holdings

### Private parade composition and compelled inclusion

**Holding and operative rule:** Applying a public-accommodations law to compel private parade organizers to include an identified contingent conveying a message they do not choose violates the First Amendment. A parade remains expression despite broad admissions, multiple themes and the absence of a single precise message; the rule does not exempt ordinary nonexpressive goods or services from nondiscrimination law.

**Authority:** Souter’s Opinion of the Court, joined by all eight other Justices; nine Justices adopt the as-applied private-expression holding.

**Controlling explanation:** The Court holds that the right to speak includes control over the composition of a private expressive presentation. Barnette prohibits compulsory affirmation, and Tornillo protects editorial choice against compelled inclusion. Turner preserves the importance of a speaker’s selection within a particular medium; its separate broadcast context is not mechanically imported here. A parade presents expression through the groups and messages its organizers choose, even if those selections are permissive or varied. GLIB’s bannered unit has its own protected message, but that protection creates no entitlement to insert it into another private speaker’s presentation. Use of public streets under a neutral permit does not convert this parade into a governmental forum. Roberts and PruneYard concern access without the same compelled alteration of expression. Independent review of intertwined expression characterizations, with credibility deference, rejects the conclusion that absence of a single theme eliminates protection. The Court invalidates only this compelled expressive inclusion, without finding every customer identity a message or requiring proof that spectators endorse each contingent.

**Precedent treatment:**

- West Virginia State Board of Education v. Barnette: applies protection against compelled expression.
- Miami Herald Publishing Co. v. Tornillo: applies editorial selection protection.
- Turner Broadcasting System, Inc. v. FCC: applies existing medium-specific editorial protection, without importing unrelated broadcast results.
- Roberts v. United States Jaycees; PruneYard Shopping Center v. Robins: distinguish access not materially altering the private expressive composition.
- Bose Corp. v. Consumers Union; Fiske v. Kansas: apply independent review of intertwined First Amendment characterizations while retaining credibility deference.

The respondents’ state-action/public-forum theory against the City was dismissed and not cross-appealed; it is not revived here. The importance of message misattribution in another setting remains open. No general antidiscrimination exemption, facial invalidation or judgment about the correct political message is announced.

#### Precedent Treatment

The treatment accompanying the holding controls. Ordinary nondiscrimination applications remain distinct and enforceable.

#### Law After Decision

Private parade composition receives protection against compelled insertion of an identified expressive unit. Other expressive-association disputes require a comparably material expressive burden; ordinary customer identity alone is not a message.

### Lebron v. National Railroad Passenger Corp., No. 93-1525. — 1995-02-21

The mandate reverses the Second Circuit judgment and remands for proceedings consistent with Amtrak's governmental status for First Amendment purposes. The lower courts must decide the unresolved constitutional and remedial issues and reconsider the associated state-law dispositions under ordinary law. The Court does not reinstate the mandatory-display injunction, set a display deadline, award damages, determine a rent amount, or resolve either side's final contract rights. Review here ends; proceedings continue below.

### McIntyre v. Ohio Elections Commission, No. 93-986 (current petitioner: executor of Margaret McIntyre's estate). — 1995-04-19

Authority: [Record](../records/McIntyre_v_Ohio_Elections_Commission_merits_1995-04-19.md#public-projection).

The Supreme Court of Ohio's judgment is reversed. Further proceedings must set aside the enforcement order and $100 fine against Margaret McIntyre, whose estate's executor maintains the challenge. Ohio may enforce valid disclosure rules consistent with the holding. The Court orders no damages, replacement election procedure, blanket statutory repeal, or retained Supreme Court proceeding.

### Adarand Constructors, Inc. v. Peña, No. 93-1841. - 1995-06-12

Authority: [Record](../records/Adarand_Constructors_Inc_v_Pena_merits_1995-06-12.md#public-projection)

The Tenth Circuit judgment remains effective. No remand is ordered by this decision, and no administrative program redesign or continuing judicial supervision is directed.

### Missouri v. Jenkins, No. 93-1823 - 1995-06-12

Authority: [Record](../records/Missouri_v_Jenkins_merits_1995-06-12.md#public-projection)

The Eighth Circuit judgments remain in force and the district court retains remedial jurisdiction under the existing orders. Missouri may seek partial or full release on a proper motion with the complete governing showing. Further changes require their own legal and factual basis. No new supervisory arrangement is ordered and no mandatory next release date is set.

### Hurley v. Irish-American Gay, Lesbian and Bisexual Group of Boston; No. 94-749 — 1995-06-19

Authority: [Record](../records/Hurley_v_Irish_American_Gay_Lesbian_and_Bisexual_Group_of_Boston_merits_1995-06-19.md#public-projection).

The Supreme Judicial Court judgment is reversed and the case remanded. The injunction compelling GLIB’s inclusion shall be dissolved. The public-accommodations law remains enforceable where its application does not compel private expression. No fee amount, fine or sanction is decided.

### Lebron v. National Railroad Passenger Corp., No. 93-1525. — 1995-02-21

Authority: [Record](../records/Lebron_v_National_Railroad_Passenger_Corp_merits_1995-02-21.md#public-projection); public substance is copied in the [generated chunk handoff](../render-inputs/OT_1994CHUNK2.md).

Justice O'Connor would affirm. Lebron preserved his First Amendment claim, but deliberately excluded the theory that Amtrak itself was a governmental entity in his presentations below. The express governmental-actor question here permits consideration of that theory; it does not require the Court to disregard the litigant's earlier choice. Her objection is prudential. She does not claim that the Court lacks jurisdiction, that the entire constitutional claim was waived, or that entity status falls outside the question presented. Nor does she find an additional concession or actual reliance prejudice on a record that establishes neither.

She would decide the conduct-attribution theory on the private-entity premise on which it was litigated. Jackson and Blum require a supported governmental connection to a private actor's particular choice, while her Edmonson dissent distinguishes such choice from governmental assistance. The reported funding, property, stockholding, and board relationships do not establish an external governmental direction causing this refusal or an exclusively governmental advertising function. The political subject of the artwork does not itself supply the missing connection. On that limited path she would leave the appellate rejection of the federal claim and disposition of the unresolved state-law matters in place. She does not convert the failure of the federal theory into a universal jurisdictional dismissal rule.

O'Connor recognizes that the permanent public governance would support the Court's structural conclusion if that alternative were reached. That conditional agreement supplies no vote for a holding she declines to adjudicate and no judgment vote for reversal. Even an affirmative actor ruling, she explains, would not itself establish the right to the district court's mandatory-display injunction; forum, speech, and equitable questions would still require decision.

### McIntyre v. Ohio Elections Commission, No. 93-986 (current petitioner: executor of Margaret McIntyre's estate). — 1995-04-19

Authority: [Record](../records/McIntyre_v_Ohio_Elections_Commission_merits_1995-04-19.md#public-projection).

Justice Thomas concurs in the judgment. Talley's protection and the historical expressive tradition it recognizes support an individual's anonymous political pamphlet. The supplied justification cannot sustain a penalty imposed solely for missing identification on this ordinary leaflet. He does not adopt the Court's modern value-balancing and content-based-scrutiny methodology as his own ground. His agreement in this judgment therefore adds no vote to the Court's complete scrutiny explanation, and his writing does not invalidate every financing or organizational disclosure rule.

Justice Scalia dissents. Election-specific attribution is a limited accountability requirement that helps voters identify a communication's source and assists enforcement against electoral wrongdoing. Buckley's disclosure principles and Bellotti's source-information discussion support that safeguard in his view. Talley's general prohibition covering all anonymous handbills is distinguishable from Ohio's election category. He recognizes the expressive burden but regards the State's justification as sufficient here; he would sustain the fine. He preserves appropriate protection where a speaker establishes threats or reprisals, without finding that McIntyre experienced either. He does not endorse a general power to forbid anonymous discussion outside the election setting. These complete published separate positions are effective April 19, 1995 and noncontrolling. Ginsburg publishes no separate opinion; her reservation is part of the Court's limits. No unverified original-era historical assertion or state-statute survey is incorporated into either writing.

### Adarand Constructors, Inc. v. Peña, No. 93-1841. - 1995-06-12

Authority: [Record](../records/Adarand_Constructors_Inc_v_Pena_merits_1995-06-12.md#public-projection)

Justice O'Connor dissents, joined by Justice Kennedy throughout and by Justices Scalia and Thomas in the uniform-scrutiny and vacatur/remand portions. She would require a compelling interest and narrow tailoring for federal explicit racial classifications, replace Metro's relaxed tier, and remand for actual application. Congress's institutional competence may inform evidence and fit, but does not replace individual judicial protection. Strict scrutiny need not invalidate every racial remedy. O'Connor and Kennedy support the dissent's particular stare-decisis explanation; it is not joined by Scalia or Thomas and is not the Court's ground.

Justice Scalia separately dissents. He supports strict review and vacatur/remand but rejects compensation or entitlement based merely on shared racial ancestry, and does not join an affirmative endorsement of group catchup remedies. Justice Thomas separately dissents, rejecting the benign/paternalistic exception and racial entitlement by group ancestry. His broader equivalence and paternalism reasoning is his own; neither separate position makes the unknown certification route invalid or issues an injunction.

Justice Souter concurs, joined by Justices Ginsburg and Breyer, emphasizing the actual scope of Fullilove and Metro, constitutional stare decisis, and the need to distinguish preserved challenges from disputed concession characterizations. Justice Ginsburg also concurs, joined by Justice Breyer, emphasizing continuing exclusion, meaningful access and Congress's remedial competence while preserving review of outsiders' burdens. These concurrences add no unrestricted quota entitlement or universal benign-program exemption.

The compensation clause is an optional incentive, distinct from STURAA's expenditure command or a mandatory subcontracting duty. DBE subcontracts must total more than 10% of the original prime contract; compensation is 10% of approved final DBE subcontract amounts, capped at 1.5% of the original contract for one DBE and 2% for two or more. It compensates the stated assistance and documentation duties and requires certification evidence and a certified executed subcontract copy. Compensation is confined to DBE-performed work; adjustment may apply to non-DBE second-tier work, with full compensation permitted where only insignificant portions are subcontracted and the DBE performs the major portion. No finding is made that every duty was performed or every payment is constitutional.

SBA §8(a) presumes social disadvantage but requires individual proof of economic disadvantage. DOT state certification rebuttably presumes both, and third parties may challenge actual disadvantage. The §8(d) regulations remain in tension: 13 CFR §124.106(b) appears to require individual economic proof while 48 CFR §19.703(a)(2) appears to presume both. Nonminority applicants may establish disadvantage. Other governmental, county or city certification is accepted individually only on the contracting officer's finding of an acceptable and viable certification program. Mandatory §8(a) review, urged but nonmandatory DOT state review, and the absence of an identified separate §8(d) review provision remain distinct. Gonzales's actual route is unknown. The Court does not find irrebuttability, lack of actual disadvantage, or resolve the conflicting §8(d) rules.

The Tenth Circuit judgment remains effective. No remand is ordered by this decision, and no administrative program redesign or continuing judicial supervision is directed.

### Missouri v. Jenkins, No. 93-1823 - 1995-06-12

Authority: [Record](../records/Missouri_v_Jenkins_merits_1995-06-12.md#public-projection)

Chief Justice Stone-Zsela concurs. He would recognize joint development with independent review as an available method for revising the continuing remedy within the court’s discretion, rather than a prescribed new supervisory institution. State and district officials and class representatives and counsel should develop proposals with meaningful opportunities for families and educators to review information, offer alternatives and identify material disagreement. Participation supplements legal representation; it does not displace administrators, create a compliance veto or substitute agreement for the governing obligation. If disagreements remain, the judge decides.

A qualified neutral reviewer may assess implementation, evidence reliability and alternatives only under applicable procedural authority, after the parties can address qualifications and conflicts. Duties, access and scope must be defined; the parties must be able to challenge the report and its evidence. The reviewer advises and the judge retains findings, enforcement and release decisions. Ex parte Peterson supports defined judicial assistance; La Buy forbids displacement of the judicial function, and neither supplies unrestricted appointment authority. Public reports and reasons should identify remaining injury, actual remedial benefits, burdens and alternatives while protecting student information. Review must remain proportionate to unresolved work.

Programs, staffing, schedules and progress measures may be revised with explained connections to the same remedial question and intelligible comparisons over time. Recruitment and retention evidence can inform salary necessity; better retention alone does not establish elimination of educational injury. An ineffective measure need not remain simply because it was in the initial decree. These are possible evidence sources, not a compulsory checklist or a general-improvement mission. Release requires the governing defendant-specific showing; neither plaintiff agreement, consensus nor reviewer endorsement is necessary. A motion cannot be indefinitely delayed by participation or review. Material evidentiary deficiencies should be identified, monitoring should narrow with remaining work, and further relief must rest on proper authority and record support. This method remains noncontrolling and adds no substantive release condition.

Justice O’Connor dissents, joined by Scalia, Kennedy and Thomas. They would remand the challenged salary orders to identify an independently sufficient intradistrict repair ground because the adopted external-recruitment purpose cannot obtain authority merely from personnel necessity to implement that objective. They acknowledge the actual educational and necessity findings, lawful support-staff funding and clear-error constraints; they would not dismantle all magnet remedies, reverse every funding order, claw back salaries or impose new taxation. For quality education, they accept complete Freeman burdens, whole-decree conduct, retained-control necessity or practicability, nonobstruction and defined obligations. They would require explicit separation of residual injury and practicable repair from contextual maximum-potential and goals-in-entirety language. Correct abstract factors and inadequate implementation proof do not, in their view, identify the independently lawful application. They find no earned entitlement to release and impose no plaintiff causation burden, national score threshold or mandatory supervisor. Their scope permits examination of the salary purpose actually presented without reopening all settled remedial foundations.

Violation-linked educational repair includes supported instructional and critical support-staff implementation. The complete Freeman rule remains controlling without a national-score-parity requirement. The broad magnet foundation is reserved on this review. The judgment does not guarantee general improvement, perpetual funding or immediate release. Shaw’s racial-design-only districting holding neither removes the independently established segregation injury nor decides school-remedy scope.

The Eighth Circuit judgments remain in force and the district court retains remedial jurisdiction under the existing orders. Missouri may seek partial or full release on a proper motion with the complete governing showing. Further changes require their own legal and factual basis. No new supervisory arrangement is ordered and no mandatory next release date is set.
